Multiparty Session Types with a Bang!
Matthew Alan Le Brun, Simon Fowler, Ornela Dardha

Abstract
Replication is an alternative construct to recursion for describing infinite behaviours in the π \pi -calculus. In this paper we explore the implications of including type-level replication in Multiparty Session Types (MPST) , a behavioural type theory for message-passing programs. We introduce MPST! , a session-typed multiparty process calculus with replication and first-class roles. We show that replication is not an equivalent alternative to recursion in MPST, and that using both replication and recursion in one type system in fact allows us to express both context-free protocols and protocols that support mutual exclusion and races. We demonstrate the expressiveness of MPST! on examples including binary tree serialisation, dining philosophers, and a model of an auction, and explore the implications of replication on the decidability of typechecking.



1
Introduction

Our world is powered by a multitude of computer systems working together by

communicating
,
i.e.,
sending and receiving messages according to some

protocol
. It is therefore vital to
verify
the correctness of both
communication protocols and their implementations, to ensure our programs behave
according to their specifications, and to guarantee that specifications are
indeed
safe
.

Session
types

[
9
,
14
,
15
,
30
]

provide a lightweight method by which a developer can ensure safety of, and
conformance to, communication protocols.
Session types can be thought of as
types for protocols
which can be
attached to a communication
channel
to specify
how
it should be
used, and can be used to detect issues such as
communication mismatches

and
deadlocks
early in the development process.

Multiparty session types

(MPST)
[
7
,
16
,
24
]

generalise binary session types to allow reasoning about communication between
two
or more
participants, and have been shown to be expressive
enough to capture a range of practical protocols such as the OAuth 2
authentication protocol
[
26
]
.

Example 1
(Client-Server-Worker)

Using generalised MPST
[
26
]
, we describe the types for three participants in a simple work-offloading system.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}{\oplus}\textsf{req}(\texttt{int})\ .\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w}}}\&\textsf{ans}(\texttt{str})}\\[4.62497pt]
\begin{array}[]{c c}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}{\&}\textsf{req}(\texttt{int})\ .\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w}}}{\oplus}\textsf{fw}(\texttt{int})}&\ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w}}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}{\&}\textsf{fw}(\texttt{int})\ .\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}{\oplus}\textsf{ans}(\texttt{str})}\end{array}\end{array}

(1)

The above describes types for a
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}
lient,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}
erver, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w}}}
orker respectively.
The
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}
lient, having type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}}
, sends (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\oplus}
) a

req
uest to the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}
erver with payload type

int
, then (
.
) waits to receive (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\&}
) an

ans
wer from the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w}}}
orker with payload type

str
.
The
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}
erver, upon receiving the

req
uest from the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}
lient,

f
or

w
ards it to the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w}}}
orker.
Lastly the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w}}}
orker, after receiving the

f
or

w
arded request from the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}
erver, sends the

ans
wer to the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}
lient.
A MPST system verifies that any written program code conforms to this
specification (known as
session fidelity
), and that this protocol is

safe
—
i.e.,
that processes send and receive messages of compatible
types.

Despite the potential of MPST for safe distributed programming, there remain
limitations to the theory that impede their adoption for practical systems.
For instance, generalising
Example

1
to multiple workers in the style
of a load-balancer is non-trivial and has inspired a series of work on the
generalisation of
direction of choice

[
27
]
.
Further, generalising the number of
clients
is also non-trivial—typically, MPST theories assume a global view of
all
participants in a session.
Lastly, the
objects
of actions in MPST (e.g., the recipient of a sent
message) are
hard coded
because role names are
constants
.

Example 2
(Load Balancer for
n
clients)

We introduce
replication
and
first-class roles
, combined with undirected choice in sends, to generalise
Example

1
to support
two workers
and
any number
of clients.

\begin{array}[]{r l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}}&:={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}{\&}\textsf{req}(\texttt{int})\ .\ {\oplus}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}\textsf{fw}(\texttt{int},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}})\ .\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}{\oplus}\textsf{wrk}({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}})\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}\textsf{fw}(\texttt{int},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}})\ .\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}{\oplus}\textsf{wrk}({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}})\end{array}\right.}\\[9.24994pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{i}}}}}&:={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}{\&}\textsf{fw}(\texttt{int},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\gamma}}})\ .\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\gamma}}}{\oplus}\textsf{ans}(\texttt{str})}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \textnormal{for }i\in\{1,2\}\end{array}

(2)

The first difference is the use of the
bang
(
!
) operator to denote a
replicated
action,
i.e.,
one which may occur
any number
of times.
This makes the server agnostic to the
number
of requests.
Second, a server now waits for requests—not from a specific client—but from
any
participant, binding the name of the sender to role variable
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
.
This makes the server agnostic to the
source
of the requests.
The server then makes a
choice
to forward the request to one of two workers, notably whilst passing the name of the client as one of the payloads.
Finally, the server informs the client of the choice it made by sending the name of the worker in that branch.
The worker type is also updated to be replicated, as it is dependent on the number of requests forwarded by the server.
Notably, it receives the name of the client in the forward message, binding it to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\gamma}}}
, and uses it to send the final answer.
A client may now be defined as:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c_{j}}}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}{\oplus}\textsf{req}(\texttt{int})\ .\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}{\&}\textsf{wrk}({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\omega}}})\ .\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\omega}}}\&\textsf{ans}(\texttt{str})}\ \ \ \ \textnormal{for any }j\in\mathbb{N}

(3)

As a result of the
replicated types
and
first-class role names

on the server-side, we may instantiate
any number of clients
and
have them make
any number of requests
—all without changing the server-side protocol.
Conversely, updating the number of workers has
no impact
on the types of clients.
Thus, this extension promotes
modular
design of components in multiparty systems.

In fact, as we will see in
Section

3
, the addition of
replication—especially when it is used in tandem with recursion—has several
surprising consequences, in particular allowing us to describe
context-free
protocols
as well as protocols that deal with
races
and
mutual exclusion
.

Contributions.

The overarching contribution of this paper is the first integration of
replication and first-class roles into a generalised MPST calculus, and
an exploration of the impacts of these extensions on expressiveness and decidability.
Our specific contributions are as follows:

1.

We present
MPST!
, the first multiparty
session-typed language with
replication
and
first-class
roles
(
Section

2
), and prove its
metatheory
in the form of
subject
reduction
and
session fidelity
properties (
Section

2.4
).

2.

We show several expressiveness results through a series of
representative examples (
Section

3.1
): in particular,
replication lifts the expressive power of types and thus we give the
first account of
context-free
MPST. We show that combining
both replication and recursion allows us to model races and
mutual exclusion; we demonstrate nontrivial examples
including
binary tree serialisation
, the
dining
philosophers problem
, and an
auction service
.

3.

We demonstrate the impacts of replication on the decidability of
typechecking (
Section

3.2
). We show that the decidability of
typechecking is contingent on the decidability of a given safety
property, and demonstrate conditions guaranteeing a property to be
decidable. Finally we show two syntactic approximations to allow us to
verify that a property is decidable.

Section

4
gives an account of related work and
Section

5

concludes.

2
Multiparty Session Types with a Bang!

In this section we introduce
MPST!
, a conservative extension of existing
multiparty session
calculi
[
7
,
24
,
26
]

with support for
replication
and
first-class roles
.

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle::={\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\ \bigm|\ x

\displaystyle(\textit{session w/ role, channel variable})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}

\displaystyle::={\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}

\displaystyle(\textit{role name value, \hbox{\pagecolor{highlight}role name variable}})

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle::=c\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\ \ \ \ V::=c\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}

\displaystyle(\textit{names, values})

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle::=x\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\ \ \ \ d::={\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}

\displaystyle(\textit{binders, concrete values})

\displaystyle P,Q

\displaystyle::=P\,|\,Q\ \bigm|\ (\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s})\,P\ \bigm|\ \bm{0}

\displaystyle(\textit{composition, restriction, termination})

\displaystyle\ \bigm|\ {\textstyle\sum_{i\in I}}c_{i}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{i}}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{m}_{i}\langle\widetilde{V_{i}}\rangle\ .\ P_{i}

\displaystyle(\textit{choice of sends})

\displaystyle\ \bigm|\ [{!}]\,c[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}]\;\&_{i\in I}\;\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{b_{i}})\ .\ P_{i}

\displaystyle(\textnormal{\hbox{\pagecolor{highlight}[\emph{replicated}]} \emph{branching receive}})

\displaystyle\ \bigm|\ \textsf{def }D\;\textsf{in }Q\ \bigm|\ \textnormal{{X}}\langle\widetilde{c}\rangle

\displaystyle(\textit{process definition, process call})

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle::=\textnormal{{X}}(\widetilde{x})=P

\displaystyle(\textit{process declaration})

Figure 1:
Syntax of
MPST!

2.1
Language

Figure

1
shows the syntax of
MPST!
.

Names, values, and binders.

A
session name
, ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s},{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s^{\prime}},\dots
,
represents a collection of interconnected participants. A
role
is a
participant in a multiparty communication protocol, and each
communication
endpoint

{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]
is obtained by indexing a session name with a role.
In contrast to existing MPST calculi,
MPST!
supports
first-class
roles,
meaning that a role may be communicated as part of a message. To this end, a role

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
may either be a concrete role

\bm{p}
(e.g.,

\bm{s}
or

\bm{w}

in our load balancing example) or a
role variable

\bm{\alpha}
.
A name
a
is either an endpoint, a variable, or a role variable, whereas
a value
V
is either a channel or a role.
Binders
b
are used when receiving a message and can either be a variable
binder or a role variable binder.
Concrete values
d
are used when sending a message (at runtime) and are either
an endpoint or a role name value.

Processes.

Processes are ranged over by
P,Q,R,\dots
: process
P\,|\,Q
denotes

P
and
Q
running in parallel;
session restriction

(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s})P
binds
session name
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}
in process
P
; and
\bm{0}
is the inactive process.

As in the
\pi
-calculus, but unlike other MPST calculi,
MPST!
supports

output-guarded choice

\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{i}}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{m}_{i}\langle\widetilde{V_{i}}\rangle\ .\ P_{i}
,
allowing a nondeterministic send along any
c_{i}
to role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{i}}}}
with
label
\textsf{m}_{i}
and payload
\widetilde{V_{i}}
, with the process continuing as
P_{i}
.

Branching receive

c[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}]\&_{i\in I}\ \textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{b_{i}})\ .\ P_{i}

denotes a process waiting on channel
c
for one of a set of messages from
role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
with label
\textsf{m}_{i}
, binding the received data to
\widetilde{b_{i}}

before continuing according to
P_{i}
.
It is key to note that the object of a communication action is indicated via

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
(for both sending and receiving), which can either be a concrete
value, or a role variable.
The second key difference is the optional use of the
bang

!
with a branching receive, marking it as
replicated
—
i.e.,
it may
be used
0
or more times, modelling
infinitely available servers
.

Process reduction

{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{m}_{k}\langle\widetilde{d}\rangle\ .\ Q\ \rightarrow\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s_{j}}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q_{j}}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{j}}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{m}_{j}\langle\widetilde{d_{j}}\rangle\ .\ P_{j}}\hskip Figure 2:
Operational semantics for the extended multiparty session
\pi
-calculus.

Definition 1
(Reduction context)

A
reduction context

\mathbb{C}
is given as:

\mathbb{C}::=\mathbb{C}\,|\,P\ \bigm|\ (\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s})\,\mathbb{C}\ \bigm|\ \textsf{def }D\;\textsf{in }\mathbb{C}\ \bigm|\ [\ ]
.

A
reduction context
allows us to evaluate processes under parallel composition and name restrictions.
With this, reduction rules on processes are given in
Figure

2
.
The rules make use
of a standard
structural congruence

\equiv
(
Appendix

0.A
) that
allows us to treat parallel composition as commutative and associative, as well
as including the usual
\pi
-calculus scope extrusion rule.

Rule
Figure

2
shows synchronous communication between two processes in
session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}
. The first process, playing role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}
, offers role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}
a
choice of message labels and associated process continuations. The second
process, playing role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}
, sends a message with label
\textsf{m}_{k}
and
transmits payloads
\widetilde{d}
. The first process reduces to the selected
continuation with the transmitted payloads substituted for the binders in the
selected branch, and the second process reduces to the continuation
Q
.

Rules
Figure

2
and
Figure

2
describe communication with a

replicated
process
R
. Rule
Figure

2
is similar to

R-C
but the replicated process remains unchanged and the continuation

Q_{k}
is evaluated in parallel. Rule
Figure

2
handles the case where
the replicated process does not need to receive from a specific role, but
instead allows communication with an
arbitrary
role: the rule binds the
sending role to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
in the replicated continuation.
We refer to this as a
universal receive
.

The
Figure

2
rule evaluates a branching output by nondeterministically
evaluating to one of the sending branches; rule
Figure

2
handles a recursive
call.
Finally, rules
Figure

2
and
Figure

2
are administrative,
allowing reduction modulo structural congruence and under contexts respectively.

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{req}\langle 42\rangle\ .\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}]\;{\&}\;\textsf{wrk}({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\omega}}})\ .\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\omega}}}]\;{\&}\;\textsf{ans}(z)\ .\ \bm{0}

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}

\displaystyle:={!}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}]\;{\&}\;\textsf{req}(x)\ .\ {\sum_{i=1}^{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{i}}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{fw}\langle x,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\rangle\ .\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{wrk}\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{i}}}}\rangle\ .\ \bm{0}

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{i}}}}

\displaystyle:={!}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{i}}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}]\;{\&}\;\textsf{fw}(y,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\gamma}}})\ .\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{i}}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\gamma}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{ans}\langle\text{``life''}\rangle\ .\ \bm{0}

Figure 3:
Process definitions for load balancer example

Example 3
(Load Balancer: Process Reduction)

We recall the load balancer example from
Section

1
, but this time, we
present processes for each role (
Figure

3
) to
demonstrate how our operational semantics handles communication.
Consider a single client
P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}
in parallel with three server-side
processes:

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\ \ \ \

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\ \ \ \

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\ \ \ \

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{req}\langle 42\rangle\ .\ P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,|\,\ \ \ \

\displaystyle{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}]\;{\&}\;\textsf{req}(x)\ .\ P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,|\,\ \ \ \

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\ \ \ \

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}

Using
Figure

2
, the client and server reduce.
The reduction advances the client to its continuation
P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}^{\prime}
, and pulls out a copy of the server’s continuation as a new process.
It is key to note that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
is acting as a binder in
P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}
, therefore, in the continuation we observe a role variable substitution:

\displaystyle\rightarrow\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle\left({\sum_{i=1}^{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{i}}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{fw}\langle x,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\rangle\ .\ P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s_{i}}}}^{\prime\prime}\right)\ \{42/x\}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{i}}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{fw}\langle 42,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}\rangle\ .\ \left(P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s_{i}}}}^{\prime\prime}\ \{42/x\}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}\right)

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}

The spawned server process will then non-deterministically choose a worker
to send to via rule
Figure

2
; suppose
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}
is picked.

\displaystyle\rightarrow\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{fw}\langle 42,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}\rangle\ .\ P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s_{1}}}}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}]\;{\&}\;\textsf{fw}(y,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\gamma}}})\ .\ P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}

Communication is now possible between the spawned server process and worker
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}
, using rule
Figure

2
.
As before, this communication advances the sender process and pulls out a copy of the worker’s continuation:

\displaystyle\rightarrow\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,|\,\ \ \ \

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\ \ \ \

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s_{1}}}}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\,|\,\ \ \ \

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\ \ \ \

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}^{\prime}\ \{42/y\}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\gamma}}}\}

\displaystyle\,|\,\ \ \ \

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}

The client can now learn which worker was chosen,
terminating the spawned server process, then the answer is exchanged
between the worker and client:

\displaystyle\rightarrow\

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}]\;{\&}\;\textsf{ans}(z)\ .\ \bm{0}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle\bm{0}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}][{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}]\;{\oplus}\;\textsf{ans}\langle\text{``life''}\rangle\ .\ \bm{0}\!

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\

\displaystyle\bm{0}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle\bm{0}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle\bm{0}

\displaystyle\,|\,\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}

\displaystyle\equiv\

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}\ \,|\,\ P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}\ \,|\,\ P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}

2.2
Types

Figure
4
shows the syntax of
MPST!
types.

Syntax of types.

Session types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}
type communication endpoints. They consist of
branching types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\&}}
,

replicated
branching types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!(S^{\&})}
,
selection types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\oplus}}
,
recursive types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.S}
and variables

t
, and the

end
type.

Branching session types have the form

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}\&_{i\in I}\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}}).S_{i}}
indicating that role

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
offers a choice of message labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{m}_{i}}
with payload types

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\widetilde{T_{i}}}
and continuations
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{i}}
.
Similarly, selection session types

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\oplus_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{i}}}}\,\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}}).S_{i}}
indicate
an internal choice towards one of a set of roles
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{i}}}}
, with a message label, given payload types and
continues as the corresponding continuation type.
A
replicated
branching type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}\&_{i\in I}\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}}).S_{i}}
types a replicated channel.
As with processes, role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
can either
be a concrete role, or a role variable in
binding
position.

Our type system supports
singleton
types for roles: role

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
has singleton type
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
, used to pattern-match
specific roles in payloads.

Static
types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}
are used for protocol
specification, whereas
runtime
types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U}
are used by the
type semantics to include a notion of
parallel composition

at type level:
originally introduced by Le Brun and Dardha
[
4
]
,
a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U_{1}\,|\,U_{2}}

allows a channel to be associated with multiple
“active” session types.

We assume that branching and selection types include a non-empty set of messages
with pairwise distinct message names
\textsf{m}_{i}
(per role for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\oplus}
). We further take an

equi-recursive
view of types, identifying a recursive type with its
unfolding (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.S}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.S}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textnormal{{t}}}\}
) and require that recursion variables are guarded.

Session types

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}

\displaystyle::={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\&}}\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{!}(S^{\&})}\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\oplus}}\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.S}\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textnormal{{t}}}\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}

Branching/Selection Types

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\&}}

\displaystyle::={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}\&_{i\in I}\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}}).S_{i}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\oplus}}::={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\oplus_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{i}}}}\,\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}}).S_{i}}

Role singletons

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}

\displaystyle::={\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}

Static types

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}

\displaystyle::={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}

Runtime types

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U}

\displaystyle::={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}(U_{1}\,|\,U_{2})}

Figure 4:
Syntax of Types

Definition 2
(Subtyping)

The
subtyping relation

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}
is co-inductively defined on types by the following inference rules:

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}(\widetilde{T_{i}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,\widetilde{T_{i}^{\prime}})_{i\in I}}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}(S_{i}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,S_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in I}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}\&_{i\in I}\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}}).S_{i}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}\&_{i\in I\cup J}\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}^{\prime}}).S_{i}^{\prime}}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}(\widetilde{T_{i}}\geqslant\widetilde{T_{i}^{\prime}})_{i\in I}}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}(S_{i}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,S_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in I}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\oplus_{i\in I\cup J}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{i}}}}\,\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}}).S_{i}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,\oplus_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{i}}}}\,\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}^{\prime}}).S_{i}^{\prime}}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\&}_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,S^{\&}_{2}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{!}S^{\&}_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,{!}S^{\&}_{2}}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,U^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,S^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U\,|\,S\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,U^{\prime}\,|\,S^{\prime}}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.S}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textnormal{{t}}}\}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,S^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.S\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,S^{\prime}}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,S^{\prime}}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.S^{\prime}}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textnormal{{t}}}\}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,\mu\textnormal{{t}}.S^{\prime}}}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,T}}

Our definition of subtyping (
Definition

2
) is mostly standard.
We adopt the convention of smaller types being ones with less external choice and more internal choice (
à la
Gay and Hole
[
13
]
).
Subtyping of replication is based on regular branching; and parallel types are related iff their session types are subtypes.
Subtypes are related up-to their recursive unfolding, and subtyping is
reflexive
.

Definition 3
(Type Congruence)

Type congruence
allows us to treat parallel runtime types as commutative and associative with identity element
end
.

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U_{1}\,|\,U_{2}\equiv U_{2}\,|\,U_{1}}}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}(U_{1}\,|\,U_{2})\,|\,U_{3}\equiv U_{1}\,|\,(U_{2}\,|\,U_{3})}}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U\,|\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}\equiv U}}

Typing contexts

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Theta}::=\emptyset\!\ \bigm|\ \!{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Theta},X\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\widetilde{S}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}::=\emptyset\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma},c\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U}\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\end{array}

Context splitting

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}

\frac{}{\emptyset=\emptyset\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,\emptyset}\quad\textsc{lab={}, right={}, vcenter}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma},c\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}},c\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}}\quad\textsc{lab={}, right={}, vcenter}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={}, Context addition

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}

vcenter}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={}, right={}, Context role insertion

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}\hookleftarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}\hookleftarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}\par{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}\hookleftarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}

Figure 5:
Typing contexts and context operations.

Figure
5
shows the definition of typing contexts
and their operations.
Context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Theta}
is used to type recursive process definitions, mapping process
variables
X
to tuples of parameter types. Context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
maps channels to
types, and role variable singletons. Context
composition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
is defined iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}

have disjoint domains. We lift subtyping and type congruence to contexts in the
usual way.

As inspired by (e.g.)
[
30
]
, linearity is
enforced through the use of a
context split
operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}

that splits a context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
into two environments

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}
. These environments may share variables with
unrestricted types and role variables. Additionally, a channel
c
with
runtime type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U_{1}\,|\,U_{2}}
may be split such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}
contains
c:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U_{1}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}
contains
c:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U_{2}}
; this allows us to type endpoints used by
different replicated processes.
The inverse operation is
context addition

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}

that combines
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}
into an environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
; again
roles may be shared across environments. In the case that we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}},c:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U_{1}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}},c:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U_{2}}
(i.e., a linear variable used in two different
contexts), context addition results in
c
having the combined runtime type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U_{1}\,|\,U_{2}}
.
The
role insertion
operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}\hookleftarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
is used when
typing replicated receives and extends a context with a mapping
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
in the case that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
is a role variable, and leaves

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}
unchanged otherwise.

Before presenting the typing rules, we introduce the notion of
a
session protocol
, mapping role names to session types.
The
\textsf{assoc}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi})
function associates a session protocol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi}
to a
concrete session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}
to form a typing context.

Definition 4
(Session Protocol)

A session protocol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi}
is attached to a session through the restriction operation in the form of
(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi})\ P
, dictating the protocol for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}
in
P
.
A protocol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi}
is a partial mapping from role to session type, given as:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi}::=\emptyset\ \bigm|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}

A protocol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi}
is well-formed iff it does not contain parallel types.
We obtain a typing context by associating roles in a protocol
with a session name.
Formally:

\textsf{assoc}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S},{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi}):={\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S},\textsf{assoc}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \textsf{assoc}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}(\emptyset):=\emptyset

Next, we introduce predicate
end
on type contexts.
This ensures that an environment is
end-typed
: that is, its session types are
congruent to type
end
.

Definition 5
(End-typed environment)

A context is
end-typed
, written
\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
, iff it
only maps channels to session type
end
.

\frac{}{\textsf{end}(\emptyset)}\par\frac{\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})}{\textsf{end}(c\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})}\par\frac{\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})}{\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\equiv}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}\ \ \textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})}{\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}})}

Figure 6:
Typing rules.

Figure
6
shows the typing rules for
MPST!
. There are three
judgements: the value typing judgement
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}\vdash V:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}

assigns type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}
to value
V
under context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}
and consists
of four rules. Rule
Figure

6
allows weakening: if value
V
has type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}
under some environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}
, and we have an end-typed
environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
, then the
rule allows us to conclude that
V
has type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}
under
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}
.
Rule
Figure

6
types concrete roles as singleton types under the empty
environment, whereas
Figure

6
types a role variable provided
that it is contained within the type environment.
Finally, rule
Figure

6

types a linear variable, allowing for subtyping. Judgement
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Theta}}\vdash X:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\widetilde{S}}
simply looks up process variable
X
in process environment

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Theta}}
, returning its parameter types; this is achieved by rule
Figure

6
.

The final judgement
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Theta}}\,;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}\vdash P
states that
process
P
is typable under recursion environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Theta}}
and type
environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}}
.
Rule
Figure

6
types the inactive process under an end-typed environment.
Rule
Figure

6
types parallel processes under a
split environment. Rule
Figure

6
types an output-directed choice; since this
operation uses branching control flow, each choice must be typable under the
same environment.

Rules
Figure

6
and
Figure

6
type replicated and non-replicated receives
respectively. In both cases the rules check that the channel has a receiving
session type. Both rules check that each branch is typable by extending a common
typing context with the variables bound by the receives, along with the
continuation type for the session channel.
In the case of a replicated receive there are two differences: first, the
context used to type each branch must be end-typed (in order to avoid
duplicating linear resources). Second, since the role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
may be a

binding
occurrence of a role variable
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
, the context used
to type each branch must be extended with the role variable (if applicable)
using the insertion operator.

Rule
Figure

6
types a session name restriction
(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi})P
.
As is standard in generalised MPST
[
26
]
, the session protocol must satisfy a
safety property

\varphi
.
We discuss specifics of this property in
Section

2.3
, and how it is used in
Section

2.4
.
Informally, the property
ensures that all process communication is “compatible”, and is the weakest
property required to prove subject reduction.
We then prove our metatheory parametric on the
largest
safety property,
allowing it to be customised to verify more precise properties (e.g., to ensure deadlock-freedom or
termination), based on the requirements of a specific protocol.

Rule
Figure

6
types an output if the sending channel
can be mapped to a selection type with payload types that match the values being sent.
The rule ensures that role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
is either a
value, or it exists in the type context—
i.e.,
messages cannot be sent to unbound role variables.
The selection type continuation should be used, along with the common context, to type the continuation process.

Lastly, rules
Figure

6
and
Figure

6
type recursive processes.
The former populates the recursion environment whilst ensuring that process declarations are well typed under the variables they bind.
The latter checks that types of values used in a process call match what was specified in the declaration.

Example 4
(Load Balancer: Type Checking)

By introducing a name restriction for session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}
that includes the
types from
Example

2
, we can type the processes from

Example

3
under empty typing contexts:

\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}}:\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}}\})\ P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}\,|\,P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}\,|\,P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}\,|\,P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}

Figure 7:
Type semantics.

To reason about the interactions between session types, following Scalas &
Yoshida
[
26
]
, we endow typing contexts
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}

with LTS semantics as shown in
Figure

7
.
Each action
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\gamma}
denotes an
output
,
input
, and

synchronising communication
respectively.

Context reduction

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
is defined iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{:}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}{,}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{:}\textsf{m}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}},\textsf{m}
,
and we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}^{*}
for the transitive and reflexive closure of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}
.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
.

Transitions
Figure

7
and
Figure

7
are standard:
a context can fire an input label (resp. output label) matching any
of the labels in the top-level branch type (resp. selection type).
This transitions the entry to the continuation of the chosen type.

Transition
Figure

7
models the receipt of a message by a replicated input.
The two main differences to the linear receive are:

(i)
the role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
used in the transition label is allowed to be a role variable name; and

(ii)
firing an input does not advance the type, but instead pulls out a copy of the continuation and places it in parallel.
Role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
is considered bound in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}R}
and its continuations, but is
free
in pulled out copies of the continuations composed in parallel (

S_{k}
).

Transition rules
Figure

7
,
Figure

7
,
Figure

7
allow contexts to reduce under a larger context, or when types are guarded by recursive binders.
Concretely,
Figure

7
allows transitions to ignore role singletons;
Figure

7
allows a context to perform a transition when split from a larger context (the transition result must be added back in); and
Figure

7
allows recursive binders to be treated equal to their unfolding.

Transitions
Figure

7
and
Figure

7
model type-level
communication; for simplicity and without loss of generality we assume a
convention wherein session-typed payloads precede role-typed payloads.
Both rules state that if a context can be split such that one part fires an output label, and the other fires an input with matching roles, message label and payloads, then the entire context can transition via a
communication
action.
We note that payloads will consist of either session types or role singletons.
For the former, sender payloads must be subtypes of the receiver payloads; for the latter,
role substitution
occurs after communication.

Figure

7
caters for universal receives, where the input label consists of a role variable in binding position—this is reflected in the role substitution.
We say that a context
reduces
iff it can transition via communication actions.

Example 5
(Load Balancer: Context Reduction)

We now use the protocol from
Example

4
to demonstrate context reduction in action.
Initially, the only communication action possible is between the client and server, via
Figure

7
.

\begin{array}[]{r l}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}},\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}},\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}},\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}}\\
=&\\
&({\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{c}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{s}}}})\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{1}}}}},\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{w_{2}}}}})\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}&\\
