\end{array}

Safety.

In order to type a session restriction, rule
Figure

6
in

Figure

6
requires that the session’s protocol of the session must
obey a
safety property

\varphi
. Informally, a safety property requires
that processes exchange values of compatible types and that a sender only ever
selects available branches. Safety is the weakest typing context property required
in order to prove subject reduction.

Definition 6
(Safety)

\varphi
is a safety property on type environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
iff:

\frac{}{\varphi\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\oplus_{i\in I}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{i}}}}\,\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{S_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\widetilde{r_{i}}}}}).S_{i}^{\prime}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\ \&_{j\in J}\,\textsf{m}_{j}(\widetilde{S_{j}^{\prime\prime}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\widetilde{\alpha_{j}}}}}).S_{j}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\right)\\
\\
\text{\ \ \ \ {and} }\exists K\subseteq I\text{ s.t. }\forall k\in K:{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{k}}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}\\
\\
\text{\ \ \ \ {implies} }K\subseteq J\text{ and }\forall i\in K:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\widetilde{S_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\widetilde{S_{i}^{\prime\prime}}}\text{ and }|{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\widetilde{r_{i}}}}}|=|{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\widetilde{\alpha_{i}}}}}|}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={S-${\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\oplus\&}$}, right={}, vcenter}\par\frac{}{\varphi\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\oplus_{i\in I}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{i}}}}\,\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{S_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\widetilde{r_{i}}}}}).S_{i}^{\prime}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{!}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{0}}}}\ \&_{j\in J}\,\textsf{m}_{j}(\widetilde{S_{j}^{\prime\prime}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\widetilde{\alpha_{j}}}}}).S_{j}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\right)\\
\\
\text{\ \ \ \ {and} }\exists K\subseteq I\text{ s.t. }\forall k\in K:{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{k}}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}\text{\ \ \ \ {and}\ \ \ \ }{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho_{0}}}}\text{ is either a variable or }{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\\
\\
\text{\ \ \ \ {implies} }K\subseteq J\text{ and }\forall i\in K:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\widetilde{S_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\widetilde{S_{i}^{\prime\prime}}}\text{ and }|{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\widetilde{r_{i}}}}}|=|{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\widetilde{\alpha_{i}}}}}|}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={S-${\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{!}{\oplus}{\&}}$}, right={}, vcenter}\par\frac{}{\varphi({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.S})\ \ \ \ \text{implies}\ \ \ \ \varphi({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.S}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textnormal{{t}}}\})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={S-${\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu}$}, right={}, vcenter}\par\frac{}{\varphi({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})\ \ \ \ \text{implies}\ \ \ \ \textsf{frv}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})=\emptyset}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={S-${\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}$}, right={}, vcenter}\par\frac{}{\varphi({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}\ \ \ \ \text{implies}\ \ \ \ \varphi({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={S-${\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}$}, right={}, vcenter}

A property
\varphi
is considered
safe
iff it conforms to all conditions specified in
Definition

6
.
Conditions
Definition

6
and
Definition

6
are concerned with communication.
They state that if two roles in the same session have an output and input type respectively which point at each other, then:

(i)

they should have at least one common message label;

(ii)

for each common label, their payloads should be equal in length; and

(iii)

for each common label, all session types in the payload of the sender should be subtypes of what is expected by the receiver.

Condition
Definition

6
requires safety to hold after the unfolding of recursive binders;
Definition

6
requires all role variables used in a context to be bound by that same context; and
Definition

6
requires safety to hold after context reduction.

Users of the type system can re-instantiate
\varphi
with custom properties (
e.g.
,
termination
), as long as the property used meets the safety requirements.

2.4
Metatheory

Unlike most session type theories,
generalised
MPST do not syntactically guarantee any properties on the processes they type.
Rather, they provide a framework for verifying runtime properties on the type context, from which process-level properties may be inferred—the benefit being that these properties are undecidable to check on processes, yet decidable in the realm of the type system.
Furthermore, its generalised nature allows for fine-tuning based on specific requirements of its applications.
Informally, generalisation of the type system works by proving the metatheory parametric of the largest safety property captured by
\varphi
in
Definition

6
;
i.e.,
all theorems proved and presented which involve a type context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
, assume that
\varphi({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
holds, or “
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}

is safe
”.
Essentially,
\varphi
describes the minimum (and most general)
safety
requirements made for
subject reduction
to hold.
This proof technique allows
\varphi
to be re-instantiated with more specific properties without having to reprove any of the base metatheory.
(We occasionally reference properties other than
safety
in examples.)

2.5
Subject Reduction and Session Fidelity

The main results of a generalised MPST system are
subject reduction
(
Theorem

2.1
) and
session fidelity
(
Theorem

2.2
).
These theorems allow the type system to be used as a framework for verifying custom properties by re-instantiating
\varphi
.
We note that discussing
how
the generalised type system can be used as a verification framework is
not
the focus of this paper (to this end, we address the interested reader to Scalas and Yoshida
[
26
, Section 5]
);
rather, we build on generalised MPST theory as a means of investigating the expressiveness of replication and first-class roles in MPST.
Hence, the following presents the two theorems—highlighting key differences
with what is standard—but we do not demonstrate the verification of
runtime properties (which is standard). We give the proofs in
Appendix

0.C
.

Theorem 2.1
(Subject Reduction)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Theta}\,;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\vdash P
with
\varphi({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
and
P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
, then
\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}^{*}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Theta}\,;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}\vdash P^{\prime}
with
\varphi({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}})
.

Intuitively, subject reduction states that any
safe
and
well-typed
process remains safe and well-typed after process reduction.
More formally, the theorem asserts that if a process is typed under a safe context, then the context can match any process reduction to type its continuation whilst retaining safety.

Session fidelity
states that if a context can reduce, then a process it types can observe
at least one
of its reductions.
By virtue of subtyping, a context is allowed to specify paths which need not be followed by a process it types; the key point is that session fidelity requires that there is
at least one
observable reduction.
Using session fidelity, one can prove properties about communication occurring within a single session.
It does not, however, provide grounds for showing such properties on interleaved session communication.
Hence, as is standard in generalised MPST, we define additional assumptions on processes required for session fidelity to hold.

Definition 7
(Only plays one role)

(The following is a slight adaptation of
[
26
, definition 5.3]
.)
Assuming
\emptyset\,;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\vdash P
, we say
P
:

1.

has guarded definitions
iff each subterm of P with the form

\textsf{def }\textnormal{{X}}((x_{i}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{i}})_{i\in 1..n})=Q\;\textsf{in }P^{\prime}

we have:
\forall i\in 1..n:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.27776pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.27776pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.125pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.70836pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}
implies a process call
\textnormal{{Y}}\langle\dots,x_{i},\dots\rangle
can only occur in
Q
as a subterm of a communication action over channel
x_{i}
.

2.

only plays role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}
, by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
iff

(i)

Item

1
holds for P;

(ii)

\textsf{fv}(P)
=
\emptyset
;

(iii)

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{0}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.27776pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 5.27776pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.125pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.70836pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}
and
\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{0}})
;

(iv)

in all subterms
(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}})\ P^{\prime}
of
P
, we have
\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}})
.

The purpose of
Item

1
is to prevent processes from infinitely
reducing via
Figure

2
without communicating,
and
Item

2
identifies a typical application of MPST where a
number of processes
P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}
communicate over a multiparty session

{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}
, with each process playing a
single
role.
The difference in our definition to the standard is that processes
P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}

should play a
single
role and not a
unique
role.
This is due to the introduction of replication in our language; note how
reduction with a replicated process is guaranteed to produce multiple processes
playing the same role and is reflected in our definition in condition

(iii)
of
Item

2
, where a channel is mapped to

runtime type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U}
, allowing for parallel composition.

Informally, the session fidelity theorem states that, given a safe context that
types a process of a particular structure—
i.e.,
one governed by the session
fidelity assumptions of
Definition

7
—then if the context can reduce,
the typed process can match at least one reduction.
Furthermore, after the process matches the context reduction, it remains within
the session fidelity assumption structure.

Theorem 2.2
(Session Fidelity)

Assume
\emptyset\,;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\vdash P
with
\varphi({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
and
P\equiv{\bigm|_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\in I}\!P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}}
where each
P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}
is either
\bm{0}
(up-to

\equiv
), or only plays role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}
.
Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}
implies
\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}},P^{\prime}
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
,
P\rightarrow^{*}P^{\prime}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}\vdash P^{\prime}
, where
P^{\prime}\equiv{\bigm|_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\in I}\!P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}^{\prime}}
and each
P_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}^{\prime}
is either
\bm{0}
(up-to
\equiv
), or only plays role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}
.

We now turn our attention to the main focus of this paper,
i.e.,
exploring the expressiveness and decidability of replication and first-class roles in MPST.

3
Expressivity and Decidability

This section discusses, and shows by example, the benefits and limitations of
MPST!
.

Section

3.1
demonstrates the exressivity gained by using replication and first-class roles in MPST,
whilst
Section

3.2
presents our decidability results.

3.1
Expressivity

We begin by demonstrating a common design pattern used for describing protocols, which we call
services
.
We build a number of services to showcase language features, and to describe protocols which—to the best of our knowledge—were previously untypable in any MPST theory.
Specifically, using the increased expressiveness of replication and first-class roles, we define types for
binary trees
, the
dining philosophers problem
, and an
auction
.
Importantly, all examples shown adhere to the decidability requirements discussed later (
cf.

Section

3.2
).

Services.

A
service
is a building-block of a protocol, involving some universal receive, with the aim of
offering
a specific interaction.
A
client
interacts with a service to achieve the communication pattern it offers.
Importantly, services may be clients of other services, promoting a modular design of protocols in
MPST!
.

Example 6
(Ping)

The
ping service
simply responds to a
ping
with a
pong
.

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\&\textsf{ping}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}{\oplus}\textsf{pong}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

A basic yet useful service is given in
Example

6
, where role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}
offers a
ping
service.
As a convention, we will use capitals for naming services.
We highlight the importance of both replication and free role names in types to be able to design modular components of a protocol—both are integral to designing a sub-protocol agnostic of the larger scope in which it is used.

Context-Free MPST.

Context-free session
types
[
28
,
1
,
21
]

are a formalism that replace prefix-style session types with individual actions,
along with a sequencing operator
;
with neutral element
skip
.
The goal of this line of work is to express communication protocols that are not
possible under tail-recursive session types, given their restriction to regular
languages.
The classic example is that of communicating a serialised binary tree.

Example 7
(Binary tree in standard context-free STs)

Consider a binary
tree
data type described by the following context-free grammar.

\textsf{tree}::=(\textsf{node},\textsf{tree},\textsf{tree})\;\ \bigm|\ \;\textsf{leaf}

We could attempt to represent a protocol that serialises this data type as
follows:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.\oplus\{\textsf{leaf}:\textsf{skip},\ \textsf{node}:\textnormal{{t}}\}}

However, this type is not precise enough—it does not guarantee that the correct structure of a binary tree is maintained.
Work on context-free session types solves this by proposing type systems allowing the following specification:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.\oplus\{\textsf{leaf}:\textsf{skip},\ \textsf{node}:\textsf{skip};\textnormal{{t}};\textnormal{{t}}\}}

Selecting the

node
label now guarantees that
two
sub-trees will follow.

The parallel types presented in
Section

2.2
, although not exposed directly to users, lift expressiveness of types in
MPST!
.
In fact, since replicated branches are
permanently available
(by composing continuation types in parallel), we can simulate the sequencing operator
;
using type-level parallel composition.

Example 8
(Binary Tree Service)

Recall the ping service
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}
from
Example

6
.
We build a
binary tree service

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{T}}}
using
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}
as shown below:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{T}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}\&\textsf{tree}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}{\oplus}\textsf{ping}\,.\,{!}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}\&\textsf{pong}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}\&\left\{\textsf{leaf}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}},\ \textsf{node}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}{\oplus}\textsf{ping}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}{\oplus}\textsf{ping}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}\right\}}

The service begins by receiving a request for a

tree
from a client.
It then sends a

ping
to the ping service, exposing a replicated branch waiting to receive the

pong
reply.
The client is now free to build the binary tree.
It is key to note that any

node
sent to the service will subsequently forward
two

ping
requests to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}
.
In turn, this communication will pull out two copies of the type continuation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}\&\{\textsf{leaf}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}},\ \textsf{node}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}{\oplus}\textsf{ping}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}{\oplus}\textsf{ping}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}\}}
, forcing the client to maintain the appropriate binary tree structure.
For example, if a client
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{t}}}
wishes to build a tree consisting of one root node and two leaf nodes, its type would be defined as:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{t}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{T}}}{\oplus}\textsf{tree}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{T}}}{\oplus}\textsf{node}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{T}}}{\oplus}\textsf{leaf}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{T}}}{\oplus}\textsf{leaf}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

The metatheoretic framework can be used to determine that any protocol failing to abide by the binary tree structure will result in a
deadlock
;
whilst any safe protocol that obeys the correct structure is
terminating
,
e.g.
the protocol
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{t}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{T}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}\}
.

An obscure limitation of the tree service in
Example

8
is that it can only be used by a single client.
Consider, for example, two separate clients sending a

node
message to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{T}}}
.
Since both tree service types communicate with
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}
to unroll the replicated branch
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}\&\textsf{pong}}
, the protocol becomes non-deterministic in a non-confluent manner and can result in deadlocked behaviour.
To resolve this issue, we amend the tree service to accept a payload role which should act as a personal ping service for the client; this guarantees that the tree type is only unrolled by the client that made the initial request.

Example 9
(Multi-Client Binary Tree)

We now redesign the binary tree service, this time capable of concurrently building multiple trees for different clients.
The key difference here being that the new service,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}
, accepts a role as a payload on the initial request to which it will issue its

ping
s.

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{!}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\&\textsf{tree}({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}})\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}{\oplus}\textsf{ping}\,.\,{!}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}\&\textsf{pong}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\&\left\{\textsf{leaf}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}},\textsf{node}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}{\oplus}\textsf{ping}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}{\oplus}\textsf{ping}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}\right\}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}\&\textsf{ping}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}{\oplus}\textsf{pong}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

Multiple clients can now issue concurrent requests to the tree service whilst maintaining safety.
A sample (terminating) protocol is that of
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{t_{1}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{t_{2}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{1}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{2}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}\}
, where
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{1}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{2}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}}
, and the types for
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{t_{1}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{t_{2}}}}
are given by:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{t_{1}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}{\oplus}\textsf{tree}({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{1}}}})\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}{\oplus}\textsf{node}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}{\oplus}\textsf{leaf}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}{\oplus}\textsf{leaf}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{t_{2}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}{\oplus}\textsf{tree}({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{2}}}})\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}{\oplus}\textsf{node}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}{\oplus}\textsf{leaf}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}{\oplus}\textsf{node}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}{\oplus}\textsf{leaf}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}{\oplus}\textsf{leaf}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

Replication vs. Recursion.

We have seen that replication and parallel composition increases the expressive power of MPST beyond that of tail-recursion.
Naturally, one might ask, “
is recursion still needed?
”
We find replication and recursion in MPST to be mutually non-inclusive—
i.e.,
both can produce protocols which
cannot
be typed under the other construct.
We have already demonstrated this in one direction with the binary tree examples; below we showcase how recursion cannot be replaced by replication.

Example 10
(Lock Service)

The lock service provides clients with a
mutex lock
.

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{L}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\theta}}}\&\textsf{lock}\,.\,\mu\textnormal{{t}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\theta}}}\&\left\{\textsf{acquire}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\theta}}}\&\textsf{release}\,.\,\textnormal{{t}},\ \textsf{done}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}\right\}}

When a client requests a lock from
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{L}}}
, a copy of the recursive continuation is exposed.
The recursive definition allows sequences of

acquire
and

release
messages to be received.
It is key to note that, whilst replication maintains a top-level branch that is permanently available to receive a message, the top-level action in a recursive definition is
not
fixed.

Copies of the continuation type of a replicated receive are executed concurrently.

Example

10
provides a service for roles to enter race-sensitive portions of a protocol, as if it were an atomic action.
We demonstrate its use by typing the dining philosophers problem.

Example 11
(Dining Philosophers)

A number of philosophers gather to eat on a round table.
Each plate is separated by a single chopstick, and a philosopher needs two chopsticks to eat.
The dining philosophers problem requires the philosophers to employ an algorithm to ensure the table does not get deadlocked.
In such a setting, we can view
chopsticks as services
and
philosophers as clients
.
Assuming a size of
n
-philosophers, we define the type for a chopstick as:

({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i}}}})_{1..n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{L}}}{\oplus}\textsf{lock}\,.\,{!}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\eta}}}\&\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{take?}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{L}}}{\oplus}\textsf{acquire}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\eta}}}{\oplus}\textsf{ok}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\eta}}}\&\textsf{give}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{L}}}{\oplus}\textsf{release}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}\\
\textsf{done}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{L}}}{\oplus}\textsf{done}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}\end{array}\right\}}

Before offering its service, a chopstick requests a lock from
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{L}}}
.
This ensures that every chopstick has its own lock that it may

acquire
and

release
.
The lock is used to guarantee that a chopstick is only ever taken by a single philosopher at a time.
A chopstick then waits for a

take?
request from a philosopher; receiving one will result in it attempting to

acquire
the lock.
This acquisition is only successful if the same role has not already requested it in some other parallel composition.
If the lock was already acquired, then the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{L}}}{\oplus}\textsf{acquire}}
will block until the lock is released.
Acquiring the lock sends an
ok
back to the philosopher,
symbolising that they have successfully obtained the chopstick.
When done from eating, the philosopher may then send back a

give
message, which in turn releases the lock, as the chopstick is now available to be taken by a different philosopher.

We can now write an algorithm for philosophers.
First, a naive approach:

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{i}}}})_{i\in 1..n}:

\displaystyle\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{take?}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i\scalebox{0.5}{$+$}1}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{take?}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i}}}}\&\textsf{ok}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i\scalebox{0.5}{$+$}1}}}}\&\textsf{ok}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{give}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i\scalebox{0.5}{$+$}1}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{give}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{fin}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}:

\displaystyle\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{1}}}}\&\textsf{fin}.\,\cdots.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{n}}}}\&\textsf{fin}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{1}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{done}.\,\cdots.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{n}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{done}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

Every philosopher
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{i}}}}
has a similar type.
They begin by requesting to take the chopsticks to their left and right—note that this results in every chopstick receiving two

take?
requests.
Receiving both

ok
messages means the philosopher can eat, and subsequently

give
back the chopsticks.
Finally, when finished, philosophers send a

fin
to role
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}
, acting as a clean-up for the protocol.
The protocol
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{i}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{L}}}\}_{i\in 1..n}
is
safe, but fails typechecking for
\varphi=\textit{terminating}
.
In fact, the naïve protocol allows for scenarios in which all philosophers take a single chopstick, resulting in a deadlock.
This problem has many solutions; we present the simplest in which philosophers take turns to eat.
(Key changes are underlined.)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{1}}

\displaystyle\,\scalebox{0.6}[1.0]{$=$}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{1}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{take?}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{2}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{take?}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{1}}}}\&\textsf{ok}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{2}}}}\&\textsf{ok}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{1}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{give}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{2}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{give}.\,\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{2}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{fin}}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{2}}

\displaystyle\,\scalebox{0.6}[1.0]{$=$}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{i\scalebox{0.4}[1.0]{$-$}1}}}}\&\textsf{fin}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{take?}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i\scalebox{0.5}{$+$}1}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{take?}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i}}}}\&\textsf{ok}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i\scalebox{0.5}{$+$}1}}}}\&\textsf{ok}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{give}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{i\scalebox{0.5}{$+$}1}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{give}.\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{i\scalebox{0.5}{$+$}1}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{fin}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{3}}

\displaystyle\,\scalebox{0.6}[1.0]{$=$}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{n\scalebox{0.4}[1.0]{$-$}1}}}}\&\textsf{fin}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{n}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{take?}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{1}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{take?}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{n}}}}\&\textsf{ok}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{1}}}}\&\textsf{ok}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{n}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{give}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{1}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{give}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{fin}.{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{n}}}}\&\textsf{fin}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{1}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{done}.\,\cdots.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{n}}}}{\oplus}\textsf{done}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

Here, all philosophers other than the first must wait for the previous to

fin
ish eating before they can request to take their chopsticks.
The updated protocol
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{1}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,S_{1}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{i}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{n}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{3}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q^{\prime}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{C_{j}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{L}}}\}^{i\in 2..n\scalebox{0.4}[1.0]{$-$}1}_{j\in 1..n}
now typechecks for
\varphi=

terminating
.

The previous examples demonstrate how recursion hidden by a universal receive can be used to mimic changes in state.
Our final example does the inverse,
i.e.,
we show how a universal receive hidden by a recursive binder can be used to model resources which eventually reach some permanent state.
In addition, we show that universal receives model
fair races
, since they do not impose an order on how communication is handled.

Example 12
(Auction)

A merchant
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{m}}}
sets up an auction
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{A}}}
to accept bids from some buyers
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{b}}}
.
A merchant can employ different mechanisms for choosing who to sell to (
e.g.
, first come first served, highest bid, biased selling,
etc.
); but must always respond to buyers with either a

yes
,

no
, or

not-avail
message.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{A}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{!}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\&\textsf{bid}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{int}}).\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{m}}}{\oplus}\textsf{bid}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{int}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}).\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}}

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{m}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{A}}}\&\textsf{bid}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{int}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}).\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}{\oplus}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{yes}.\,{!}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{A}}}\&\textsf{bid}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{int}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\kappa}}}).\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\kappa}}}{\oplus}\textsf{not-avail}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}\\
\textsf{no}.\,\textnormal{{t}}\end{array}\right\}}

({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{b_{i}}}})_{i\in 1..n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{A}}}{\oplus}\textsf{bid}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{int}}).\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{m}}}\&\left\{\textsf{yes}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}},\ \textsf{no}.\,\textnormal{{t}},\ \textsf{not-avail}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\textsf{{end}}}\right\}}

Buyers
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{b_{i}}}}
race to send

bid
s to the auction service.
In turn, the auction forwards bids and buyer role identifiers to the merchant, who processes bids sequentially (but still in an arbitrary order).
If the merchant declines a bid, then the client is offered another chance; if the merchant accepts a bid, then it exposes a replicated receive which informs any further buyers that the product is no longer available.
It is key to note that, unlike in
Example

11
where we used locks to avoid race conditions, races here are not only allowed but are integral to the protocol.
Additionally, by uncovering a replicated receive, the merchant enters a
permanent
state.
These two characteristics guarantee that, no matter the selling algorithm employed by the merchant:

(i)
bids always arrive in a fair arbitrary order; and

(ii)
the product can only be sold once.

Discussion.

We have now shown that
replication
and
recursion
are mutually
non-inclusive, and that our extension increases the expressiveness of
MPST.
It is important to understand the dependencies between added features and
the expressiveness gained.
Since
MPST!
is a
conservative
extension, it is guaranteed that
the increase of expressiveness derives from our two extensions:

1.
the addition of
replication
; and

2.
the addition of
first-class roles
.

Replication alone is enough to increase the expressiveness of MPSTs w.r.t.
the Chomsky hierarchy.
We note that,
e.g.
,
Example

8
could be easily re-written
without the universal receive, especially since it should not be used
by multiple clients to uphold deadlock-freedom—thus,

replication in MPSTs increases their expressiveness to that of
context-free languages
.

First-class roles in our formalism refers to:

(i)

universal receives
acting as binders on role variables; and

(ii)
the ability to
pass roles
as payloads in messages.
Universal receives allow
protocols to be designed agnostic of the client pool

(consider
Examples

2
,
6
,
9
and
10
);
and also act as a
fair way of introducing races
—
e.g.

Example

2

describes a load balancer that responds to requests in
no particular order
.
Role passing allows for
safe distributed choice
.
In a load balancer (in general), it is
impossible
for a client to know
which worker will service its request.
In
Example

2
, role passing allows the server to inform clients of its choice,
and also informs the worker of the identity of the client.
Role passing increases expressiveness by introducing dependencies into a protocol.
For instance,
Example

12
uses role passing to ensure the merchant correctly
services the right buyers; without it, a merchant could not respond to requests without bias.

As a final note, first-class roles are different to,
e.g.
,
delegation
or
multiple sessions
(both supported in
MPST!
) since they act
inside
a session.
Therefore our system can still be used to check for properties such as deadlock-freedom,
which is not possible with interleaved sessions without other mechanisms such as an
interaction typing system
[
3
]
or
priorities
[
8
]
.

3.2
Decidability

As one may expect, the added expressiveness of replication and first-class roles into types does not come without a cost.
Unlike the base theory that this work extends, even though our language models synchronous communication, typechecking may be
undecidable
in the general case.
In the following, we discuss decidability of typechecking in detail.
We show that typechecking is only as decidable as the safety property; we provide examples of types that make typechecking problematic; and we provide strategies for determining whether a protocol is captured by a decidable subset of
MPST!
.

Theorem 3.1
(Decidablility of type checking)

If
\varphi
is decidable, then typechecking is decidable.

Proof

Since typing rules in
Figure

6
can be deterministically applied
based on the structure of a process
P
, and a typing context need only be
split a finite number of times to separate all linear types,
there are a finite number of contexts that can be tried for each rule that requires a context split.
Lastly, subtyping is decidable
[
13
]
(decidability of subtyping replicated types is equivalent to regular branching types, and of parallel types is equivalent to checking multiple session types); and
\varphi
is decidable by assumption. ∎

Theorem

3.1
states that decidability of type checking is only as decidable as property
\varphi
.
In
Example

14
, we will demonstrate why
\varphi
may not necessarily be decidable in the general case for the type semantics presented in
Figure

7
.
To do this, we first define
behavioural sets
of type contexts (as in
[
25
, appendix K]
).

Definition 8
(Behavioural set)

The behavioural set of a type context, written
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
, is given by
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})=\textsf{unf}^{*}(\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}\,|\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}^{*}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}\})
; where
\textsf{unf}^{*}
is the closure of
unf
—a function that unfolds all top-level recursive binders in a set of contexts.
(Full definitions of
unf
and
\textsf{unf}^{*}
are standard and given in
Appendix

0.D
.)

Informally, the behavioural set of a context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
is the set of
(i)
its reductions; and
(ii)
its reductions’ unfoldings.
The benefit of
beh
is that it mechanically abides by conditions
Definition

6
and
Definition

6
from
Definition

6
.
Therefore, to determine whether
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is a safety property, all that is required is to exhaustively check the contexts that inhabit
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
against the remaining conditions of
Definition

6
.

Example 13

Consider a context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.\textnormal{{t}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t${}^{\prime}$}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\&\textsf{m}.\textnormal{{t${}^{\prime}$}}}\}
.
The behavioural set of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
is given by:

\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})=\left\{\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.\textnormal{{t}}},\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t${}^{\prime}$}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\&\textsf{m}.\textnormal{{t${}^{\prime}$}}}\end{array}\right\},\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.\mu\textnormal{{t}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.\textnormal{{t}}},\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\&\textsf{m}.\mu\textnormal{{t${}^{\prime}$}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\&\textsf{m}.\textnormal{{t${}^{\prime}$}}}\end{array}\right\}\right\}

Notice that the left element is the original context after
0
reduction
steps, whereas the right element is the unfolding of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
.
Moreover, any further reductions only yield contexts (and unfoldings) already captured by these two elements.

The next example context is problematic for typechecking.

Example 14

Consider a context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.\textnormal{{t}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\&\textsf{m}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}}\}
.
The behavioural set of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
is given by:

\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.\textnormal{{t}}},\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\&\textsf{m}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}}\end{array}\right\},\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.\mu\textnormal{{t}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.\textnormal{{t}}},\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\&\textsf{m}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}}\end{array}\right\},\\
\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\mu\textnormal{{t}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.\textnormal{{t}}},\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\&\textsf{m}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}\,|\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}}\end{array}\right\},\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.\mu\textnormal{{t}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.\textnormal{{t}}},\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\&\textsf{m}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}\,|\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}}\end{array}\right\},\cdots\end{array}\right\}

Indeed,
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is
infinite
.
This is a result of how replication and parallel composition are modelled in
Figure

7
.
In fact, the type semantics for replicated communication allows for context reduction to yield
larger
types.
Note how in this example, the contexts that inhabit
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
get infinitely larger by pulling out infinitely many copies of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}}
.

Furthermore, we point out that infinite behavioural sets are not only a result of recursive communication with replicated branches.
Consider,
e.g.
a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
=
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\&\textsf{m}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}\&\textsf{m}^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}}\}
.
Such a context will also pull out infinitely many copies of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}}
, because the replicated communication forms an infinite loop.
Lastly, it is key to note that
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}})
is finite for any
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}
that does not contain replicated branches, since there is no other way for a context reduction to yield a larger type.

Knowing whether
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is (in-)finite is key for our main decidability result.

Theorem 3.2
(Decidability of
beh
)

Let
\varphi=\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
. If
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is finite, then
\varphi
is decidable.

Proof

Since
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
contains all possible reductums and unfoldings of

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
, then conditions
Definition

6
and
Definition

6
are
satisfied immediately.
Therefore, to determine whether
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is a safety property, we may
exhaustively check all inhabitants of
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
against conditions

Definition

6
,
Definition

6
,
Definition

6
, which is
decidable since
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is finite (by assumption); and since subtyping
and
frv
are decidable. ∎

Theorem

3.2
states that
\varphi
is
decidable
for any
\varphi=\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
where
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is a finite set.
In other words, if a protocol can be shown to have a finite behavioural set, then typechecking for that protocol is
decidable
.
This could be done manually for each protocol;
however, to further increase the practicality of our type system,
we present two strategies for restricting protocols into a subset
of
MPST!
with finite behavioural sets.

Decidability Strategies.

The strategies we present for restricting protocols to decidable subsets of
MPST!
all follow a similar blueprint.
Essentially, we wish to establish properties on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
with decidable
approximations that imply that
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is finite.
Then, by Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
we obtain decidable typechecking.

The following defines, and gives examples, of each strategy; then, we show these strategies are sound and discuss how they can be approximated.

Definition

9
prevents types like
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
in
Example

14
using
a naïve approach; put simply,
tf
captures protocols where all clients of a
replicated server are intrinsically non-recursive and non-replicated.

Definition 9
(Trivially finite)

A context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
is trivially finite,
\textsf{tf}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
, iff:

1.

no type in the body of a recursive binder sends to a replicated branch; and

2.

continuations of replicated branches do not send to other replicated branches.

Example 15

The protocols modelling the dining philosophers problem in
Example

11
are
trivially finite
.
Note how the chopstick services make the initial request to the lock service
before
they offer their replicated branch.

For other protocols we need a more nuanced strategy.

Definition

10
formalises “loops” in a protocol which may result from replicated
servers infinitely bouncing messages amongst each other (such as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
in
Example

14
).

Definition 10
(Loop free)

Given a protocol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi}
, and a context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
derived from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi}
(possibly after a number of reductions),
a
cycle
in the LTS of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
is defined as the series of transitions s.t.,
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}\&_{i\in I}\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}}).S_{i}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{:}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{,}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}{:}\textsf{m}_{k}}}}\;\!\!\left(\!\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{:}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p^{\prime}_{j}}}}{,}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q^{\prime}_{j}}}}{:}\textsf{m}^{\prime}_{j}}}}\;\!\right)_{j\in 1..n}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{:}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{,}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}{:}\textsf{m}_{k}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}

where
k\in I
, and for any
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p^{\prime}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q^{\prime}}}},\textsf{m}^{\prime},n,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}
.
A cyclic replicated communication path (
CRCP
) is defined as a cycle with these added conditions:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}])={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}}
s.t. either
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{\equiv}\,!S^{\&}\,|\,U}
or

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}}
appears after a recursive binder in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi}({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}})
, for any
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\&}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U}
; and

2.

\forall x\in 1..n:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{:}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}{,}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}{:}\textsf{m}_{k}}}}\;\left(\!\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{:}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p^{\prime}_{l}}}}{,}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q^{\prime}_{l}}}}{:}\textsf{m}^{\prime}_{l}}}}\;\!\right)_{l\in 1..x-1}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{:}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p^{\prime}_{x}}}}{,}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q^{\prime}_{x}}}}{:}\textsf{m}^{\prime}_{x}}}}\;

we have:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime\prime}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q^{\prime}_{x}}}}])={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q^{\prime}_{x}}}}}
s.t. either
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q^{\prime}_{x}}}}{\equiv}\,!S^{\&}\,|\,U}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q^{\prime}_{x}}}}}
appears after a recursive binder in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Psi}({\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q^{\prime}_{x}}}})
.

We say
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
is
loop free
, written
\textsf{lf}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
, iff the LTS of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}

does not contain a CRCP.

Essentially, a
cycle
in the LTS of a context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
:

(i)
starts with an incoming communication action into a replicated type;

(ii)
performs some intermediary transitions; and

(iii)
ends with the transition that began the cycle.
A CRCP is a special case of a cycle, where all intermediary transitions must also be
between roles that have a replicated type; or form part of the body of a recursive type.
Finally, a context is
loop free
iff its LTS does not produce any CRCPs.

Example 16

Contexts
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
from
Example

14
contain CRCPs:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
contains a CRCP at
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}
with
0
intermediary transitions; and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
contains two CRCPs, at
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}
, both with
1
intermediary transition
forming part of a replicated type.

Example 17

The protocols in
Examples

2
,
8
and
9
are
loop free
:
Example

2
because there are no cycles;
Examples

8
and
9
because the cycle between the
pong
branch on
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{T}}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{M}}}
) and the
ping
branch on
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{P}}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{1}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p_{2}}}}
) includes communication with the (non-replicated/-recursive) client; breaking the CRCP.

Proposition 1

If
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is infinite, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
contains a CRCP.

Proof

From the type semantics (
Figure

7
), we observe that the only reductions that can yield
larger
types are communications with replicated branches.
Therefore, it follows that for
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
to be infinite, there must be some
reoccurring transitions in the LTS of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
that repeatedly communicates with
a replicated branch—
i.e.,
there is a communication action on a replicated branch,
followed by any number of intermediary transitions which then end with the initial
communication action on the replicated branch; where all intermediary transitions
must be non-finite.
This is the definition of a CRCP (
Definition

10
). ∎

Theorem 3.3
(Strategy soundness)

Given a context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
,
\Phi({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
implies
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is finite, for
\Phi\in\{\textsf{tf},\textsf{lf}\}

Proof

Case

tf
. By contradiction: assume
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is infinite, then, by
Proposition

1
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
contains a CRCP;
but, by
Definition

10
, a CRCP will violate at least one of the conditions for
tf
in
Definition

9
—contradiction.

Case

lf
. By contradiction: assume
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is infinite, then, by
Proposition

1
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
contains a CRCP—contradiction;
therefore
\textsf{beh}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
is
finite
.
∎

Approximations.

Properties
tf
and
lf
are both
decidable
for all protocols
without

first-class roles:

tf
can be determined via a linear traversal of a type context; and

lf
can be checked by constructing a directed graph of visited replicated
branches in a context, then checking that the graph is acyclic (which is
decidable).
An approximation is only required for protocols using role variables, since their
values can only be known at runtime.
This approximation would treat any role variable in a selection type to have the
capability of reaching
any other role
.

Example 18
(Approximation false negative)

Consider the following protocol:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\&\textsf{m}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}^{\prime}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}\&\textsf{m}^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}\&\textsf{m}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}\&\textsf{m}^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\beta}}}{\oplus}\textsf{m}}

Although the above is
trivially finite
(
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}
do not communicate),
it would be
falsely
flagged as
not

tf
because
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
is over-approximated to include
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{r}}}
.

It is key to note that false negatives of the approximation are avoidable by

requiring unique branching labels on replicated types
.
Furthermore, even with the approximation, all examples presented in this paper (except
Example

12
) are captured by either
lf
or
tf
.
With respect to
Example

12
, the presented protocol still yields a
finite behavioural set, and thus by
Theorem

3.2
and
Theorem

3.1
,
typechecking it is decidable.
We aim to continue exploring further strategies (especially ones which can capture protocols such as
Example

12
) in future work.

4
Related Work

The MAG
\pi
calculus
[
19
]
makes use of generalised MPST
theory in order to type
failure-prone
communications
(i.e., message loss,
reordering, and delays). Key to their approach is the use of timeouts to
detect and handle message loss; as with related approaches (e.g., Barwell et
al.
[
2
]
), this often means that session types
are made more complex in order to handle each potential failure point.
Our approach is most closely related to that of Le Brun &
Dardha
[
4
]
, who introduce MAG
\pi
! as a modification
of MAG
\pi
to incorporate type-level replication: this has the advantage of
simplifying client-server interactions by only requiring
clients
to
handle potential failures.
However, the aims of our work and that of Le Brun &
Dardha
[
4
]
are significantly different: while their
aim is specifically to use replication as a methodology to simplify failure
handling, our work is a more fundamental study of the consequences of
type-level replication on expressiveness and decidability.
In particular we make use of a more standard base MPST calculus (i.e., a
calculus that does not include undirected receives, nor rules that
model failures and message reordering), and we make use of
synchronous

communication semantics. Nevertheless our calculus allows for more
interesting use of replication: unlike MAG
\pi
! we allow
nested
replication
and
recursion
, whereas MAG
\pi
! only allows replication at
the top-level and processes must be finite.
Furthermore, as a result of our inclusion of
first-class roles
, as well
as using replication
in tandem with
recursion, we can type
protocols that make non-trivial use of mutual exclusion and races,
all of which would be
inexpressible in MAG
\pi
!.

Toninho & Yoshida
[
29
]
assess the relative
expressiveness of a multiparty session calculus and a process calculus inspired
by classical linear logic, showing that MPST calculi allow strictly more
expressive process networks (i.e., those that can include cycles). As part of
this investigation they explore a limited form of type-level replication that
permits a liveness property. However, their system does not consider first-class
roles and pre-dates generalised MPST so is guided primarily by global types, and
is therefore less expressive than
MPST!
.

Replicated session types have been used to a limited extent in a
wide variety of works on binary session types
(e.g.,
[
6
,
8
,
5
,
31
]
),
primarily in works pertaining to Curry-Howard interpretations of propositions as session types, where the exponential modality from linear
logic
!A
is typically linked to replication from the
\pi
-calculus.
Several further lines of work investigate client-server communication following this
correspondence.
Kokke et al.
[
18
]
investigate an extension of the logically-inspired
HCP calculus
[
17
]
with two dual modalities
!_{n}A
and
?_{n}A
to
type a pool of
n
clients and a replicated server that can service
n
requests
respectively, and show that their calculus allows nondeterministic behaviour
while still preventing deadlocks and ensuring termination.
Qian et al.
[
22
]
develop
CSLL

(client-server linear logic) that uses the dual
coexponential
modalities
¡
A
and ¿
A
to type servers and client pools
respectively, along with rules to merge client pools.
The subtle difference between the ¡
A
modality and the
exponential
!A
being that the former (informally) serves type
A

only as many
times as required according to client requests
.
This is similar to how our type system operates, given that replicated receives
only pull out copies of continuations upon communication.
Multiple requests induce non-determinism into further reductions;
in our work this is observed through parallel types, which in the work of Qian et
al.
[
22
]
is observed through
hyperenvironments
[
17
,
12
]
.
Unlike all these works, we focus on
multiparty
session types,
where interacting with a replicated channel
spawns a process that
remains in the same session
.
This results in our key novelty,
i.e.,
our account of replication in the

type semantics
with the use of parallel types.

Marshall & Orchard
[
20
]
investigate the
effects of adding a
semiring graded necessity
modality (a generalisation
of the
!
modality) to a session-typed
\lambda
-calculus,
showing interesting consequences such as replicated servers and multicast
communication.
We posit that the two systems can
type different protocols:
while
MPST!
cannot straightforwardly encode multicast
communication, it is difficult to see how their approach would scale to the
examples we describe in
Section

3
.

Rocha & Caires
[
23
]
introduce CLASS, a process calculus with a
correspondence to Differential Linear Logic
[
11
]
. CLASS integrates
session-typed communication, reference cells with mutual exclusion, and
replication. Their calculus guarantees preservation and progress, the proof of
the latter property requiring a logical relation. CLASS can encode the dining
philosophers problem, making essential use of shared state; in contrast our
implementation relies on the interplay between replication and recursion.

Deniélou et al.
[
10
]
introduce
parameterised MPST as a means of designing protocols for parallel
algorithms.
Their formalism allows for parameterisation of participants in the
form of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{client}}}[i]
, representing the
i^{\text{th}}
client from some
group of
n
clients, for a bound
n
.
The key difference between this formalism and
MPST!
is that our
approach preserves, and allows for the fair handling of,
races
.
Parameterised MPST enforce a predetermined prioritisation on the order
of communication (thus,
Example

12
cannot be expressed in
that system).

5
Conclusion

We presented
MPST!
, a conservative extension of the standard
multiparty session
\pi
-calculus which introduces for the first time

replication
and
first-class roles
, and proved its metatheory.
We have shown that the interplay between replication and recursion
allows us to describe interesting and previously inexpressible MPST protocols
such as those that rely on races and mutual exclusion, as well as giving a
method by which we can express context-free protocols.
Although replication can have implications for decidability of typechecking, we
have identified sufficient conditions that can determine decidability and
provided syntactic approximations for decidability.
For future work, it would be interesting to investigate an extension of
MPST!
with polymorphism, as this would improve on the modular design of protocols
already promoted by the type system.
Furthermore, we wish to continue exploring the decidability of typechecking
to find more general approximations.

Acknowledgements.

We thank the anonymous reviewers for their detailed and insightful comments.
This work was supported by EPSRC Grants EP/X027309/1 (Uni-pi) and EP/T014628/1
(STARDUST).

Appendix 0.A
Structural Congruence

Definition 11
(Structural Congruence)

\frac{}{P\,|\,Q\equiv Q\,|\,P}\par\frac{}{(P\,|\,Q)\,|\,R\equiv P\,|\,(Q\,|\,R)}\par\frac{}{P\,|\,\bm{0}\equiv P}\par\frac{}{(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s})\,\bm{0}\equiv\bm{0}}\par\frac{}{(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s})(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s^{\prime}})\,P\equiv(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s^{\prime}})(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s})\,P}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}\not\in\textsf{fs}(P)}{(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s})\,(P\,|\,Q)\equiv P\,|\,(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s})\,Q}\par\frac{}{\textsf{def }\textnormal{{X}}(\widetilde{x})=P\;\textsf{in }\bm{0}\equiv\bm{0}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}\not\in\textsf{fs}(P)}{\textsf{def }\textnormal{{X}}(\widetilde{x})=P\;\textsf{in }(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s})\,Q\equiv(\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s})\,(\textsf{def }\textnormal{{X}}(\widetilde{x})=P\;\textsf{in }Q)}\par\frac{\textsf{dpv}(D)\cap\textsf{fpv}(Q)=\emptyset}{\textsf{def }D\;\textsf{in }(P\,|\,Q)\equiv(\textsf{def }D\;\textsf{in }P)\,|\,Q}\par\frac{(\textsf{dpv}(D)\cup\textsf{fpv}(D))\cap\textsf{dpv}(D^{\prime})=(\textsf{dpv}(D^{\prime})\cup\textsf{fpv}(D^{\prime}))\cap\textsf{dpv}(D)=\emptyset}{\textsf{def }D\;\textsf{in }(\textsf{def }D^{\prime}\;\textsf{in }P)\equiv\textsf{def }D^{\prime}\;\textsf{in }(\textsf{def }D\;\textsf{in }P)}

Appendix 0.B
Lemmata

0.B.1
Context Operations

Lemma 1

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}\ \textit{implies}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
.

Proof

Holds by induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}
.
Notably, in the cases where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}},c\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U}
, we observe that context composition ‘
,
’ implies that
c\not\in\textsf{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}})
.
Thus, we may directly apply the context addition rules to obtain the original context. ∎

0.B.2
Substitution

Lemma 2

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}
.

Proof

By induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{2}}
.

Case
\emptyset
:
\emptyset=\emptyset\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,\emptyset\implies\emptyset\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}=\emptyset\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,\emptyset\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}
.

Case
cL
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}},c:{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}U}
and

Lemma 3

If
\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
.

Proof

By rule induction on
\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
, observing that for any context which is end-typed, there are no elements that can be substituted. ∎

Lemma 4

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\vdash V\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}
.
Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}\vdash V\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}
.

Proof

The lemma holds trivially if
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
does not occur free in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
is free in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}
, then there are two cases to consider.
First, when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}={\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
.

From the assumption and rule
figure

6
we obtain
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
.
We must show that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}
, which holds by rules
figure

6
and
figure

6
.

The second case is when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}T}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}
in which
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
occurs free.
From assumption and inversion of rule
figure

6
, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}=c\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\prime}}
,
V=c
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}S}
.
Hence, we must prove that
(c\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\prime}})\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}\vdash c\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}
.
By the definition of
frv
, this is the same as proving
c\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\prime}}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\})\vdash c\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}
.
In turn, by rule
figure

6
, we must prove that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}S}
implies
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\prime}}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}
, which holds by
lemma

7
. ∎

(by (

) and ind. hyp.)

(18)

\therefore
from lines (

)–(

) and rule
figure

, we obtain the thesis. ∎

0.B.3
Subtyping

Lemma 7

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}S^{\prime}}
implies
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\prime}}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}\}
.

Proof

By coinduction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}S^{\prime}}
.
We expand on the branching case below;
other cases follow similar reasoning.

Consider the case where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}\&_{i\in I}\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}}).S_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}\&_{i\in I\cup J}\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}^{\prime}}).S_{i}^{\prime}}
. We know:

\displaystyle\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}(\widetilde{T_{i}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,\widetilde{T_{i}^{\prime}})_{i\in I}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}(S_{i}^{\prime\prime}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,S_{i}^{\prime\prime\prime})_{i\in I}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}\&_{i\in I}\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}}).S_{i}^{\prime\prime}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}\&_{i\in I\cup J}\textsf{m}_{i}(\widetilde{T_{i}^{\prime}}).S_{i}^{\prime\prime\prime}}}

(by assumption)

(19)

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
, then the substitution takes place on both sides, and thus subtyping is preserved top-level.
By the coinductive hyp. we know that the property holds for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}(S_{i}^{\prime\prime}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\,S_{i}^{\prime\prime\prime})_{i\in I}}
, and for all payload types that are session types.
For any payload types that are
not
session types, we know by definition of subtyping (
definition

2
) that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\widetilde{T_{i}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\widetilde{T_{i}^{\prime}}}
.
Then, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\widetilde{T_{i}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\alpha}}}
, the substitution takes place on both sides, preserving the subtype relation. ∎

\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})

, then

.

By induction on
\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma})
, follows immediately from
definition

5
.

0.B.5
Safety

Lemma 14

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
is safe, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
is safe.

Proof

By contradiction. Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
not safe.
Then, by
definition

6
condition
definition

6
,
there is a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}^{*}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}
either violates
definition

6
or
definition

6
(possibly after some applications of
definition

6
).
But by
figure

7
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\cdot}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}^{*}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
that is not safe.
Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
violates
definition

6
and is itself not safe—contradiction.
Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
is safe.∎

Lemma 15

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\oplus}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\&}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}

Lemma 16

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}
is safe,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}
.
Then,
\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}

Proof

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{p}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\oplus}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}S_{\&}^{\prime}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}

Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime}}

, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}
only contains singletons (and hence
\textsf{end}({\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{\rho}}}}})
).

By
lemma

15
, we know
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\rightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
. Lastly, we obtain
{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\leqslant}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}{\color[rgb]{0,0.21,0.79}\Gamma}^{\prime\prime}}
since

(i)
for entries

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.9}s}[{\color[rgb]{1,0.1,0.14}{\bm{q}}}]
, we observe from
definition
